    "newString": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants. Pilot gaps (20--34$\\times$) exceed final $N$\\,=\\,50 estimates (8--17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}) due to smaller sample size.}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nEach subsection kills",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nEach subsection kills one alternative hypothesis via causal exclusion: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}~turn conditioning, \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad}~gradient direction conflict, leaving exposure bias as the primary remaining explanation consistent with current evidence (\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift}). \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity; \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms cross-architecture generalization.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$); e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, a convergence pattern reminiscent of attention collapse in deep transformers \\citep{dong2021attention}, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}; training dynamics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-training-ext}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning; the structure thus arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Direction Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this direction conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane, should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing gradient direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the primary cause. PCGrad eliminates direction conflict but does not address magnitude imbalance (NTP dominates GAR by 7.5--14.7$\\times$); the worsening under PCGrad may partly reflect projection side-effects rather than confirming that direction conflict is entirely absent as a factor. Cross-model analysis confirms this pattern: Llama-3.1-8B gradients are near-orthogonal (cos\\,=\\,+0.005) with 25.7$\\times$ NTP magnitude dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:gradient-crossmodel}).\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, evidence that the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; per-configuration AR trajectories in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed); exposure bias thus disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_multiseed.pdf}\n  \\caption{Multi-seed AR GAR under scheduled sampling ($\\beta$\\,=\\,0 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, three seeds). Steering nearly eliminates seed variance (std: 0.047$\\to$0.001).}\n  \\label{fig:ss-multiseed}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20, 4 turns). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) / 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (coefficient of variation, CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%); we report medians: 0.018 (TG and LoRA-only), 0.009 (Base), yielding gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}; per-turn values in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-perturn-detail}). Gate profiles differ qualitatively (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793) while Llama dips at late layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), an architecture-dependent pattern, but convergence toward identity is universal (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures, consistent with exposure bias as architecture-independent.\n\nScheduled sampling confirms cross-model generalization: Llama SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$27$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (TF GAR 0.477, AR GAR 0.166), weaker than Qwen's ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3). The phase transition is architecture-dependent: $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 triggers recovery on Qwen SS (0.593) but not Llama SS (0.173); Llama requires $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 (GAR 0.791, exceeding Qwen SS 0.717; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama-ss}). Cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B confirms the same pattern (79.8\\% GAR decay, $\\beta$-steering recovery to 0.715; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-1.5b}).\n\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. TF evaluation uses IID UltraChat and 50 held-out conversations; AR evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,50 held-out conversations (mean 3.6 user turns). All fine-tuned variants converge under TF ($\\leq$2\\,pp spread); the 8--17$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all configurations. $\\bar{g}\\!\\pm\\!\\sigma_g$: learned gate mean and std across 28 layers (LoRA-only has no gates). GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:five-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{AR ($N$\\,=\\,50)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-9}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & 95\\% CI & $\\bar{g}\\!\\pm\\!\\sigma_g$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Baseline}} \\\\\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.019 & {\\scriptsize[.014,\\,.024]} & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Gated variants}} \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & \\textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & 0.50 & \\textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & \\textbf{0.064} & {\\scriptsize[.041,\\,.091]} & .93\\,{\\scriptsize$\\pm$\\,.05} \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.030 & {\\scriptsize[.023,\\,.038]} & --- \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.49 & 0.01 & 2.78 & \\textbf{0.51} & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.033 & {\\scriptsize[.025,\\,.043]} & .96\\,{\\scriptsize$\\pm$\\,.05} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Ungated}} \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.037 & {\\scriptsize[.027,\\,.050]} & 1.00 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct with LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora,dettmers2023qlora} on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat \\citep{ding2023ultrachat} training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; dataset statistics in Appendices~\\ref{sec:appendix-data} and~\\ref{sec:appendix-dataset-ext}; the controlled comparison design is described in \\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and perplexity (PPL).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:five-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\\leq$2\\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. IID and held-out protocols produce identical rankings (held-out GAR-ours differs by $\\leq$1\\,pp), ruling out data overlap as a confound. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02) with no TF-AR gap, indicating that only concentrated last-token attention is vulnerable to distribution shift; scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{subsec:ss}).\n\nUnder AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}). The ordering (ThreadGate 0.064 $>$ LoRA-only 0.037 $>$ Uniform Gate 0.033 $>$ PCGrad 0.030 $>$ Base 0.019) shows ThreadGate vs.\\ LoRA-only is significant ($p$\\,=\\,0.004; Appendices~\\ref{sec:appendix-stats} and~\\ref{sec:appendix-stats-ext}), yet the gap remains 7.8$\\times$. A second seed confirms TF stability ($0.504\\pm0.001$). MMLU 5-shot accuracy remains within 0.8\\,pp across all variants (per-category breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-mmlu-ext}). We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as a proximal cause (9\\% PPL cost at $\\beta$\\,=\\,3; phase transition at $\\beta \\in [2, 3]$).}\n  \\label{fig:steering-recovery}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nAll training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} + \\beta \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[j \\in \\mathcal{G}],\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero. The response is sharply nonlinear: $\\beta \\in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\\,=\\,0.556 (9\\% PPL cost). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\\beta \\in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering-recovery}; full results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering}; per-$\\beta$ turn-level trajectories in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering-viz}). GAR recovery under steering is partly by construction: boosting attention logits toward goal tokens mechanically increases the attention-based metric. The stronger evidence is the counterfactual: if the collapse were due to hidden-state drift rather than attention misallocation, reweighting could not recover GAR, yet it does, consistent with attention misallocation as a proximal cause (qualitative examples in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-examples}). Downstream, steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3) raises late-turn instruction compliance by +11.1\\,pp, though this effect does not reach conventional significance ($p$\\,=\\,0.077; content-constrained tasks benefit most, +23.8\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).\n\n\\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\\label{subsec:ss}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_gap_compression.pdf}\n  \\caption{Scheduled sampling compresses the TF-AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$, the only variant approaching parity across five training conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:ss-gap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nScheduled sampling (SS; \\citealp{bengio2015scheduled}), which mixes model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ss}), directly targets the training-inference mismatch. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises to $0.401\\pm0.047$, compressing the gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ (TF GAR $0.498\\pm0.004$) with no PPL cost ($1.441\\pm0.001$; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-gap}; per-seed details in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-perseed}). Steering on SS models nearly eliminates seed variance ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: $0.717\\pm0.001$, std 0.047$\\to$0.001; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-multiseed}). SS generalizes across GAR definitions (mid-turn GAR above 0.36 for all token-position variants; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-robust}; extended robustness in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-ext}) and across architectures: on Llama-3.1-8B, SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$27$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$, weaker than Qwen likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3; \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model}). The benefit diminishes in longer dialogues (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), consistent with exposure bias compounding over turns.\n\n\n",

Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and perplexity (PPL).\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:five-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\\leq$2\\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} + \\beta \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[j \\in \\mathcal{G}],\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero. The response is sharply nonlinear: $\\beta \\in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\\,=\\,0.556 (9\\% PPL cost). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\\beta \\in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering-recovery}; full results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering}; per-$\\beta$ turn-level trajectories in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering-viz}). GAR recovery under steering is partly by construction: boosting attention logits toward goal tokens mechanically increases the attention-based metric. The stronger evidence is the counterfactual: if the collapse were due to hidden-state drift rather than attention misallocation, reweighting could not recover GAR, yet it does, consistent with attention misallocation as a proximal cause (qualitative examples in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-examples}). Downstream, steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3) raises late-turn instruction compliance by +11.1\\,pp, though this effect does not reach conventional significance ($p$\\,=\\,0.077; content-constrained tasks benefit most, +23.8\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).\n\n\\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\\label{subsec:ss}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_gap_compression.pdf}\n  \\caption{Scheduled sampling compresses the TF-AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$, the only variant approaching parity across five training conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:ss-gap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nScheduled sampling (SS; \\citealp{bengio2015scheduled}), which mixes model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ss}), directly targets the training-inference mismatch. Steering on SS models nearly eliminates seed variance ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: $0.717\\pm0.001$, std 0.047$\\to$0.001; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-multiseed}). SS generalizes across GAR definitions (mid-turn GAR above 0.36 for all token-position variants; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-robust}; extended robustness in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-ext}) and across architectures: on Llama-3.1-8B, SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$27$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$, weaker than Qwen likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3; \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model}).     "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants. Pilot gaps (20--34$\\times$) exceed final $N$\\,=\\,50 estimates (8--17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}) due to smaller sample size.}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nEach subsection kills one alternative hypothesis via causal exclusion: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}~turn conditioning, \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad}~gradient direction conflict, leaving exposure bias as the primary remaining explanation consistent with current evidence (\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift}). \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity; \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms cross-architecture generalization.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$); e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, a convergence pattern reminiscent of attention collapse in deep transformers \\citep{dong2021attention}, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}; training dynamics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-training-ext}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning; the structure thus arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Direction Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this direction conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane, should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing gradient direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the primary cause. PCGrad eliminates direction conflict but does not address magnitude imbalance (NTP dominates GAR by 7.5--14.7$\\times$); the worsening under PCGrad may partly reflect projection side-effects rather than confirming that direction conflict is entirely absent as a factor. Cross-model analysis confirms this pattern: Llama-3.1-8B gradients are near-orthogonal (cos\\,=\\,+0.005) with 25.7$\\times$ NTP magnitude dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:gradient-crossmodel}).\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, evidence that the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; per-configuration AR trajectories in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed); exposure bias thus disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_multiseed.pdf}\n  \\caption{Multi-seed AR GAR under scheduled sampling ($\\beta$\\,=\\,0 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, three seeds). Steering nearly eliminates seed variance (std: 0.047$\\to$0.001).}\n  \\label{fig:ss-multiseed}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20, 4 turns). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) / 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (coefficient of variation, CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%); we report medians: 0.018 (TG and LoRA-only), 0.009 (Base), yielding gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}; per-turn values in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-perturn-detail}). Gate profiles differ qualitatively (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793) while Llama dips at late layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), an architecture-dependent pattern, but convergence toward identity is universal (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures, consistent with exposure bias as architecture-independent.\n\nScheduled sampling confirms cross-model generalization: Llama SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$27$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (TF GAR 0.477, AR GAR 0.166), weaker than Qwen's ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3). The phase transition is architecture-dependent: $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 triggers recovery on Qwen SS (0.593) but not Llama SS (0.173); Llama requires $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 (GAR 0.791, exceeding Qwen SS 0.717; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama-ss}). Cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B confirms the same pattern (79.8\\% GAR decay, $\\beta$-steering recovery to 0.715; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-1.5b}).\n\n",

In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants. Pilot gaps (20--34$\\times$) exceed final $N$\\,=\\,50 estimates (8--17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}) due to smaller sample size.}"

        "content": "\\section{Analysis}\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[!t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}\n  \\caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\\,=\\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants. Pilot gaps (20--34$\\times$) exceed final $N$\\,=\\,50 estimates (8--17$\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}) due to smaller sample size.}\n  \\label{fig:gate-shift}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nEach subsection kills one alternative hypothesis via causal exclusion: \\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}~turn conditioning, \\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad}~gradient direction conflict, leaving exposure bias as the primary remaining explanation consistent with current evidence (\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift}). \\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity; \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms cross-architecture generalization.\n\n\\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}\n\nWe compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \\emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \\times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$); e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, a convergence pattern reminiscent of attention collapse in deep transformers \\citep{dong2021attention}, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\\% attenuation; Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}; training dynamics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-training-ext}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\\,=\\,0.777, L27\\,=\\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning; the structure thus arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?\n\n\\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Direction Conflict Exclusion}\\label{subsec:pcgrad}\n\nNTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\\times$ to 14.7$\\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this direction conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane, should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \\emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.\n\nPCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \\emph{worsens}: 0.064$\\to$0.030 ($p$\\,=\\,0.002; Figure~\\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). By \\emph{modus tollens}: removing gradient direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the primary cause. PCGrad eliminates direction conflict but does not address magnitude imbalance (NTP dominates GAR by 7.5--14.7$\\times$); the worsening under PCGrad may partly reflect projection side-effects rather than confirming that direction conflict is entirely absent as a factor. Cross-model analysis confirms this pattern: Llama-3.1-8B gradients are near-orthogonal (cos\\,=\\,+0.005) with 25.7$\\times$ NTP magnitude dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:gradient-crossmodel}).\n\n\\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\\label{subsec:dist-shift}\n\nThe TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:five-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, evidence that the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention. The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\\% per-turn decay; Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; per-configuration AR trajectories in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\nWe test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed); exposure bias thus disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\\times$) vs.\\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_multiseed.pdf}\n  \\caption{Multi-seed AR GAR under scheduled sampling ($\\beta$\\,=\\,0 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, three seeds). Steering nearly eliminates seed variance (std: 0.047$\\to$0.001).}\n  \\label{fig:ss-multiseed}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nWe replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\\,=\\,20, 4 turns). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) / 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (coefficient of variation, CoV\\,$>$\\,100\\%); we report medians: 0.018 (TG and LoRA-only), 0.009 (Base), yielding gaps of 27$\\times$ and 26$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}a in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama}; per-turn values in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-perturn-detail}). Gate profiles differ qualitatively (Figure~\\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\\,=\\,0.793) while Llama dips at late layers (L25\\,=\\,0.719), an architecture-dependent pattern, but convergence toward identity is universal (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures, consistent with exposure bias as architecture-independent.\n\nScheduled sampling confirms cross-model generalization: Llama SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$27$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$ (TF GAR 0.477, AR GAR 0.166), weaker than Qwen's ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3). The phase transition is architecture-dependent: $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 triggers recovery on Qwen SS (0.593) but not Llama SS (0.173); Llama requires $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 (GAR 0.791, exceeding Qwen SS 0.717; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-llama-ss}). Cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B confirms the same pattern (79.8\\% GAR decay, $\\beta$-steering recovery to 0.715; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-1.5b}).\n\n",
        "numLines": 43,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 43
      }
    },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. TF evaluation uses IID UltraChat and 50 held-out conversations; AR evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,50 held-out conversations (mean 3.6 user turns). All fine-tuned variants converge under TF ($\\leq$2\\,pp spread); the 8--17$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all configurations. $\\bar{g}\\!\\pm\\!\\sigma_g$: learned gate mean and std across 28 layers (LoRA-only has no gates). GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:five-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{AR ($N$\\,=\\,50)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-9}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & 95\\% CI & $\\bar{g}\\!\\pm\\!\\sigma_g$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Baseline}} \\\\\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.019 & {\\scriptsize[.014,\\,.024]} & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Gated variants}} \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & \\textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & 0.50 & \\textbf{0.02} & 2.78 & \\textbf{0.064} & {\\scriptsize[.041,\\,.091]} & .93\\,{\\scriptsize$\\pm$\\,.05} \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.030 & {\\scriptsize[.023,\\,.038]} & --- \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.49 & 0.01 & 2.78 & \\textbf{0.51} & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.033 & {\\scriptsize[.025,\\,.043]} & .96\\,{\\scriptsize$\\pm$\\,.05} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{10}{l}{\\emph{Ungated}} \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.037 & {\\scriptsize[.027,\\,.050]} & 1.00 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct with LoRA \\citep{hu2022lora,dettmers2023qlora} on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat \\citep{ding2023ultrachat} training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; dataset statistics in Appendices~\\ref{sec:appendix-data} and~\\ref{sec:appendix-dataset-ext}; the controlled comparison design is described in \\S\\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}, and perplexity (PPL).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[!t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time $\\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as a proximal cause (9\\% PPL cost at $\\beta$\\,=\\,3; phase transition at $\\beta \\in [2, 3]$).}\n  \\label{fig:steering-recovery}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:five-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\\leq$2\\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. IID and held-out protocols produce identical rankings (held-out GAR-ours differs by $\\leq$1\\,pp), ruling out data overlap as a confound. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02) with no TF-AR gap, indicating that only concentrated last-token attention is vulnerable to distribution shift; scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{subsec:ss}).\n\nUnder AR generation ($N$\\,=\\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\\times$ gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:headline}). The ordering (ThreadGate 0.064 $>$ LoRA-only 0.037 $>$ Uniform Gate 0.033 $>$ PCGrad 0.030 $>$ Base 0.019) shows ThreadGate vs.\\ LoRA-only is significant ($p$\\,=\\,0.004; Appendices~\\ref{sec:appendix-stats} and~\\ref{sec:appendix-stats-ext}), yet the gap remains 7.8$\\times$. A second seed confirms TF stability ($0.504\\pm0.001$). MMLU 5-shot accuracy remains within 0.8\\,pp across all variants (per-category breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-mmlu-ext}). We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nAll training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\\ell,h)} + \\beta \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[j \\in \\mathcal{G}],\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero. The response is sharply nonlinear: $\\beta \\in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\\,=\\,0.556 (9\\% PPL cost). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\\beta \\in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Figure~\\ref{fig:steering-recovery}; full results in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering}; per-$\\beta$ turn-level trajectories in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-steering-viz}). GAR recovery under steering is partly by construction: boosting attention logits toward goal tokens mechanically increases the attention-based metric. The stronger evidence is the counterfactual: if the collapse were due to hidden-state drift rather than attention misallocation, reweighting could not recover GAR, yet it does, consistent with attention misallocation as a proximal cause (qualitative examples in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-examples}). Downstream, steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3) raises late-turn instruction compliance by +11.1\\,pp, though this effect does not reach conventional significance ($p$\\,=\\,0.077; content-constrained tasks benefit most, +23.8\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).\n\n\\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\\label{subsec:ss}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ss_gap_compression.pdf}\n  \\caption{Scheduled sampling compresses the TF-AR gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$, the only variant approaching parity across five training conditions.}\n  \\label{fig:ss-gap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nScheduled sampling (SS; \\citealp{bengio2015scheduled}), which mixes model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-ss}), directly targets the training-inference mismatch. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises to $0.401\\pm0.047$, compressing the gap from 7.8$\\times$ to ${\\sim}$1.3$\\times$ (TF GAR $0.498\\pm0.004$) with no PPL cost ($1.441\\pm0.001$; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-gap}; per-seed details in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-perseed}). Steering on SS models nearly eliminates seed variance ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5: $0.717\\pm0.001$, std 0.047$\\to$0.001; Figure~\\ref{fig:ss-multiseed}). SS generalizes across GAR definitions (mid-turn GAR above 0.36 for all token-position variants; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-robust}; extended robustness in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-gar-ext}) and across architectures: on Llama-3.1-8B, SS compresses the gap from ${\\sim}$27$\\times$ to 2.9$\\times$, weaker than Qwen likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\\ 3; \\S\\ref{subsec:cross-model}). The benefit diminishes in longer dialogues (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), consistent with exposure bias compounding over turns.\n\n\n",

      }

2×2 grid for double-column figure ─────────────────────\nfig = plt.figure(figsize=(7.0, 2.8))\ngs = gridspec.GridSpec(2, 2, hspace=0.55, wspace=0.35,\n                       height_ratios=[1, 0.85])\n\nax_gar5  = fig.add_subplot(gs[0, 0])\nax_ppl5  = fig.add_subplot(gs[1, 0])\nax_gar50 = fig.add_subplot(gs[0, 1])\nax_ppl50 = fig.add_subplot(gs[1, 1])\n\nXLO, XHI = -0.8, 11.3\n\n# ── Shared plotting helpers ───────────────────────────────────────\ndef style_ax(ax):\n    ax.grid(axis='y', alpha=0.08, linewidth=0.5)\n\ndef plot_gar(ax, beta, gar, gar_std, title):\n    style_ax(ax)\n    ax.axvspan(2, 5, alpha=0.07, color=C_SWEET, zorder=0)\n    ax.axhline(y=0.535, color=C_GRAY, linestyle=':', linewidth=0.7, zorder=1)\n    ax.errorbar(beta, gar, yerr=gar_std, fmt='-o', color=C_TEAL,\n                markersize=4.5, markerfacecolor=C_TEAL, markeredgecolor='white',\n                markeredgewidth=0.5, capsize=2, capthick=0.7, elinewidth=0.7,\n                zorder=3)\n    ax.set_ylabel('Mid-GAR', color=C_TEAL, fontsize=9)\n    ax.set_ylim(-0.02, 1.08)\n    ax.set_xlim(XLO, XHI)\n    ax.tick_params(axis='y', colors=C_TEAL)\n    ax.set_title(title, pad=6, loc='left', fontsize=9.5, fontweight='medium')\n\ndef plot_ppl(ax, beta, ppl, ppl_std):\n    style_ax(ax)\n    ax.axvspan(2, 5, alpha=0.07, color=C_SWEET, zorder=0)\n    ax.errorbar(beta, ppl, yerr=ppl_std, fmt='--^', color=C_ROSE,\n                markersize=4, markerfacecolor=C_ROSE, markeredgecolor='white',\n                markeredgewidth=0.5, capsize=2, capthick=0.7, elinewidth=0.7,\n                zorder=3, linewidth=1.1)\n    ax.set_ylabel('PPL', color=C_ROSE, fontsize=9)\n    ax.set_xlim(XLO, XHI)\n    ax.tick_params(axis='y', colors=C_ROSE)\n    ax.set_xlabel(r'$\\beta$ (Steering Strength)', fontsize=9)\n\n# ── Panel (a): N=5 ────────────────────────────────────────────────\nplot_gar(ax_gar5, gen_beta, gen_gar, gen_gar_std,\n         '(a) $N$=5 Generation')\nax_gar5.set_xticks([0, 1, 2, 5, 10])\nax_gar5.set_xticklabels([])\nax_gar5.text(8.5, 0.56, 'TF baseline', fontsize=6.5, color=C_GRAY,\n             va='bottom', ha='center')\nax_gar5.text(3.5, 1.03, 'sweet spot', fontsize=6, color='#B8960F',\n             ha='center', va='top', fontstyle='italic', alpha=0.65)\n\nplot_ppl(ax_ppl5, gen_beta, gen_ppl, gen_ppl_std)\nax_ppl5.set_xticks([0, 1, 2, 5, 10])\nax_ppl5.set_ylim(0.9, 5.5)\n\n# ── Panel (b): N=50 ───────────────────────────────────────────────\nplot_gar(ax_gar50, gen50_beta, gen50_gar, gen50_gar_std,\n         '(b) $N$=50 Generation')\nax_gar50.set_xticks([0, 2, 3, 4, 5, 10])\nax_gar50.set_xticklabels([])\nax_gar50.text(9.5, 0.56, 'TF baseline', fontsize=6.5, color=C_GRAY,\n              va='bottom', ha='center')\nax_gar50.text(3.5, 1.03, 'sweet spot', fontsize=6, color='#B8960F',\n              ha='center', va='top', fontstyle='italic', alpha=0.65)\n\nax_gar50.annotate(r'$\\beta$=3 operating pt.',\n                  xy=(3, 0.556), xytext=(7.0, 0.15),\n                  fontsize=7, color='#444444', ha='center',\n                  arrowprops=dict(arrowstyle='->', color=C_GRAY,\n                                  lw=0.7, connectionstyle='arc3,rad=-0.12'),\n                  bbox=dict(boxstyle='round,pad=0.25', facecolor='white', alpha=0.9, linewidth=0.5))\n\nplot_ppl(ax_ppl50, gen50_beta, gen50_ppl, gen50_ppl_std)\nax_ppl50.set_xticks([0, 2, 3, 4, 5, 10])\nax_ppl50.set_ylim(0.9, 5.5)\n\nsave_fig(fig, 'fig5_attention_steering')\n",

      "- claude: Catch-all for any task that doesn't fit a more specific agent. FleetView's default when no agent name is typed. (Tools: *)",
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks. When you are searching for a keyword or file and are not confident that you will find the right match in the first few tries use this agent to perform the search for you. (Tools: *)",
      "- Plan: Software architect agent for designing implementation plans. Use this when you need to plan the implementation strategy for a task. ```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	\subsection{Preliminaries}\label{sec:background}
4	
5	\paragraph{Goal Accessibility Ratio.}
6	Standard metrics such as perplexity and attention entropy capture generation quality but not \emph{what} the model attends to. In multi-turn dialogue, a model may generate fluent text while progressively ignoring task specifications in the system prompt. We need a metric that directly measures whether goal-relevant context receives attention. To formalize this, we denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \emph{goal tokens} $\mathcal{G}$. \citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\tau$ as
7	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar}
8	  \text{GAR}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H \cdot |\mathcal{R}_\tau|} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{R}_\tau} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)},
9	\end{equation}
10	where $\mathcal{R}_\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\ell$, head $h$.
11	
12	\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}
13	\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. However, averaging dilutes the signal with intermediate positions that may legitimately attend to local context rather than the system prompt. The last token of each assistant turn is where the model aggregates information for the next continuation; attention at this position most directly reflects the model's awareness of task specifications. \textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement accordingly:
14	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar-ours}
15	  \text{GAR\text{-}ours}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{t_\tau,\, j}^{(\ell,h)},
16	\end{equation}
17	where $t_\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ (\S\ref{sec:loss}). Empirically, GAR-Dongre is diluted by NTP attention patterns to ${\sim}$0.01--0.02 in multi-turn settings, losing discriminative power across configurations; GAR-ours retains an order-of-magnitude range (0.019--0.50). The contrast itself is informative: goal attention concentrates at the last-token position, and only this concentrated pattern is vulnerable to distribution shift (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). Throughout this work, GAR serves primarily as a \emph{diagnostic probe}: it measures whether auxiliary attention training transfers to autoregressive generation, rather than being an end-to-end optimization objective whose increase directly improves downstream task performance (the correlation with instruction retention is weak; $r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
18	
19	\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}
20	
21	\begin{figure}[t]
22	  \centering
23	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}
24	  \caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under AR generation ($N$\,=\,5 conversations, 10 turns). All fine-tuned variants cluster together (77--81\% decay; $\Delta$\,=\,3.3\,pp between ThreadGate and LoRA-only); Base decays 87.5\%. Gating adds zero incremental effect.}
25	  \label{fig:attention-heatmap}
26	\end{figure}
27	
28	Different transformer layers serve different roles: early layers capture local syntax while later layers integrate long-range context \citep{gromov2024unreasonable}. A global intervention would uniformly scale all layers, but goal attention decay is layer-dependent: some layers contribute more to cross-turn information routing than others. ThreadGate therefore adds a \emph{per-layer} gating mechanism to the residual stream, allowing the model to learn which layers to modulate. At each layer $l$, the residual update is scaled by a scalar gate:
29	\begin{equation}
30	  x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l),
31	\end{equation}
32	where $f_l$ denotes the sublayer computation (self-attention or feed-forward network, FFN). Since residual networks can be viewed as ensembles of shallow paths \citep{veit2016residual}, per-layer gating selectively modulates individual paths within this ensemble. This resembles ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, which initializes a single trainable scalar at zero to stabilize deep network training, but ThreadGate differs in three ways: (i)~gates are \emph{content-conditioned}, a function of the hidden state rather than a fixed scalar; (ii)~gates are \emph{turn-conditioned}, incorporating conversation-position features; (iii)~gates are initialized near identity ($\sigma(1.5) \approx 0.82$) rather than zero, preserving the pretrained model's computation.
33	
34	Concretely, each gate $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer MLP that takes both content and turn features as input:
35	\begin{align}\label{eq:gate}
36	  g_l = \sigma\bigl(W_2\,\text{ReLU}(&W_1\,[\text{proj}(x_l);\;\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)] \notag\\
37	  &+ b_1) + b_2\bigr),
38	\end{align}
39	where $\text{proj}(\cdot)$ reduces the hidden dimension to 16, and $\text{emb}(t_d, t_i)$ provides a 16-dimensional turn embedding encoding turn distance $t_d$ (turns since system prompt) and turn index $t_i$ via learned lookup tables. The 32-dimensional input passes through a 64-unit hidden layer to a scalar gate in $(0, 1)$ via sigmoid. For Qwen-2.5-7B ($L\!=\!28$), this adds ${\sim}$57K parameters ($<$0.001\% of 7B). To isolate the contribution of turn conditioning, the \textbf{Uniform Gate} ablation replaces $\text{emb}(\cdot)$ with a learnable constant, removing turn awareness while retaining content-conditioned gating. To preserve pretrained behavior, gate biases are initialized to 1.5 (${\sim}$82\% pass-through) rather than zero as in ReZero, and gate parameters receive a $10\times$ learning rate multiplier to accelerate adaptation.
40	
41	\subsection{Training Objective}\label{sec:loss}
42	
43	Next-token prediction alone provides no gradient signal for attention allocation; a model can achieve low perplexity while distributing attention arbitrarily. The auxiliary GAR loss provides explicit pressure to maintain goal-token attention, while a diversity regularizer prevents gate collapse. The training objective combines three terms:
44	\begin{equation}
45	  \mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \lambda_\text{GAR}\,\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} + \lambda_\text{div}\,\mathcal{L}_\text{div}.
46	\end{equation}
47	$\mathcal{L}_\text{NTP}$ is the standard next-token prediction loss. For $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$, we use MSE rather than cross-entropy because GAR is a continuous ratio and MSE provides \emph{bidirectional} pressure, penalizing both under- and over-attention and preventing identity collapse (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-loss}):
48	\begin{equation}
49	  \mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} = \text{MSE}(\text{GAR\text{-}ours}, \tau),
50	\end{equation}
51	where $\tau = 0.5$ targets allocating half of the last token's attention to goal tokens. Without diversity pressure, all gates converge near 1.0; the gate diversity loss prevents this:
52	\begin{equation}
53	  \mathcal{L}_\text{div} = \text{ReLU}(\sigma_\text{min} - \text{std}(\mathbf{g})),
54	\end{equation}
55	where $\mathbf{g} = [g_1, \ldots, g_L]$ and $\sigma_\text{min} = 0.05$ is a minimum standard deviation threshold ($\lambda_\text{GAR} = \lambda_\text{div} = 1.0$). This formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-loss}): cumulative (v1--v2) and maximize (v3--v6) objectives either starve gate gradients at equilibrium or drive gates toward identity; only MSE provides the bidirectional pressure needed to prevent identity collapse.
56	
57	\subsection{Controlled Comparison Design}\label{sec:comparison-design}
58	
59	We design a five-way comparison where each adjacent pair isolates one factor: \textbf{ThreadGate (TG) vs.\ Uniform Gate (UG)} (turn conditioning), \textbf{TG vs.\ LoRA-only} (gating mechanism), \textbf{ThreadGate vs.\ +PCGrad} (gradient conflict via gradient surgery \citep{yu2020pcgrad}), and \textbf{Fine-tuned vs.\ Base} (LoRA adaptation). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}).
60	
61	
```

28	
29	\subsection{Setup}
30	
31	We fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct with LoRA \citep{hu2022lora,dettmers2023qlora} on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat \citep{ding2023ultrachat} training split. Training uses 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\,GB; training costs in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-cost}). Full hyperparameters are in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}; dataset statistics in Appendices~\ref{sec:appendix-data} and~\ref{sec:appendix-dataset-ext}; the controlled comparison design is described in \S\ref{sec:comparison-design}. We evaluate under \emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours, GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention}, and perplexity (PPL).
32	
33	\begin{figure*}[!t]
34	  \centering
35	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}
36	  \caption{Inference-time $\beta$-steering recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, confirming attention misallocation as a proximal cause (9\% PPL cost at $\beta$\,=\,3; phase transition at $\beta \in [2, 3]$).}
37	  \label{fig:steering-recovery}
38	\end{figure*}
39	
40	\subsection{Main Results}
41	
42	Table~\ref{tab:five-way} presents the five-way comparison. Under TF evaluation, all fine-tuned variants converge: GAR-ours spans 0.49--0.51 ($\leq$2\,pp), PPL is 2.78 for all. IID and held-out protocols produce identical rankings (held-out GAR-ours differs by $\leq$1\,pp), ruling out data overlap as a confound. GAR-Dongre remains low (0.01--0.02) with no TF-AR gap, indicating that only concentrated last-token attention is vulnerable to distribution shift; scheduled sampling breaks this pattern (\S\ref{subsec:ss}).
43	
44	Under AR generation ($N$\,=\,50), GAR-ours drops to 0.019--0.064, an 8--17$\times$ gap (Figure~\ref{fig:headline}). The ordering (ThreadGate 0.064 $>$ LoRA-only 0.037 $>$ Uniform Gate 0.033 $>$ PCGrad 0.030 $>$ Base 0.019) shows ThreadGate vs.\ LoRA-only is significant ($p$\,=\,0.004; Appendices~\ref{sec:appendix-stats} and~\ref{sec:appendix-stats-ext}), yet the gap remains 7.8$\times$. A second seed confirms TF stability ($0.504\pm0.001$). MMLU 5-shot accuracy remains within 0.8\,pp across all variants (per-category breakdown in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-mmlu-ext}). We analyze this distribution shift in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
45	
46	\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\label{subsec:steering}
47	
48	All training-time interventions leave the exposure bias gap intact. If the collapse is caused by attention misallocation rather than representation degradation, then directly correcting attention should recover GAR. We test this by adding a constant bias $\beta$ to pre-softmax attention logits for goal-token positions during generation:
49	\begin{equation}
50	  \tilde{A}_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)} = A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)} + \beta \cdot \mathbf{1}[j \in \mathcal{G}],
51	\end{equation}
52	where $\tilde{A}$ denotes the modified logits before softmax. The additive form operates in log-probability space, providing uniform boost even when baseline attention is near zero. The response is sharply nonlinear: $\beta \in [0, 2]$ yields marginal improvement, then $\beta = 3$ triggers a phase transition to GAR\,=\,0.556 (9\% PPL cost). At $\beta$\,=\,5, GAR reaches 0.727$\pm$0.061 (PPL 1.77); the operating region $\beta \in [3, 5]$ exceeds the TF baseline while PPL remains below 1.8 (Figure~\ref{fig:steering-recovery}; full results in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-steering}; per-$\beta$ turn-level trajectories in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-steering-viz}). GAR recovery under steering is partly by construction: boosting attention logits toward goal tokens mechanically increases the attention-based metric. The stronger evidence is the counterfactual: if the collapse were due to hidden-state drift rather than attention misallocation, reweighting could not recover GAR, yet it does, consistent with attention misallocation as a proximal cause (qualitative examples in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-examples}). Downstream, steering ($\beta$\,=\,3) raises late-turn instruction compliance by +11.1\,pp, though this effect does not reach conventional significance ($p$\,=\,0.077; content-constrained tasks benefit most, +23.8\,pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ir}).
53	
54	\subsection{Scheduled Sampling Compresses the TF--AR Gap}\label{subsec:ss}
55	
56	\begin{figure}[t]
57	  \centering
58	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_ss_gap_compression.pdf}
59	  \caption{Scheduled sampling compresses the TF-AR gap from 7.8$\times$ to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$, the only variant approaching parity across five training conditions.}
60	  \label{fig:ss-gap}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	Scheduled sampling (SS; \citealp{bengio2015scheduled}), which mixes model-generated tokens into training prefixes with linearly increasing probability (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ss}), directly targets the training-inference mismatch. Across three seeds, AR GAR-ours rises to $0.401\pm0.047$, compressing the gap from 7.8$\times$ to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ (TF GAR $0.498\pm0.004$) with no PPL cost ($1.441\pm0.001$; Figure~\ref{fig:ss-gap}; per-seed details in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-perseed}). Steering on SS models nearly eliminates seed variance ($\beta$\,=\,5: $0.717\pm0.001$, std 0.047$\to$0.001; Figure~\ref{fig:ss-multiseed}). SS generalizes across GAR definitions (mid-turn GAR above 0.36 for all token-position variants; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gar-robust}; extended robustness in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gar-ext}) and across architectures: on Llama-3.1-8B, SS compresses the gap from ${\sim}$27$\times$ to 2.9$\times$, weaker than Qwen likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\ 3; \S\ref{subsec:cross-model}). The benefit diminishes in longer dialogues (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-n5}), consistent with exposure bias compounding over turns.

```text
1	\section{Analysis}\label{sec:analysis}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[!t]
4	  \centering
5	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}
6	  \caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift ($N$\,=\,5 pilot): the TF-AR GAR gap persists across all five variants. Pilot gaps (20--34$\times$) exceed final $N$\,=\,50 estimates (8--17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:five-way}) due to smaller sample size.}
7	  \label{fig:gate-shift}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Each subsection kills one alternative hypothesis via causal exclusion: \S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}~turn conditioning, \S\ref{subsec:pcgrad}~gradient direction conflict, leaving exposure bias as the primary remaining explanation consistent with current evidence (\S\ref{subsec:dist-shift}). \S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms selectivity; \S\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms cross-architecture generalization.
11	
12	\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}
13	
14	We compare gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers. The gates invert the design intent: in 27/28 layers, gate values are \emph{lower} at turn~1 than turn~21 (Wilcoxon $p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$, $r = 0.975$); e.g., layer~23 shifts from 0.769 to 0.979. NTP pressure pushes gates toward 1.0, a convergence pattern reminiscent of attention collapse in deep transformers \citep{dong2021attention}, yielding inference-time values of 0.93--0.98 (2--7\% attenuation; Table~\ref{tab:five-way}; full gate profiles in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gate-profile}; training dynamics in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-training-ext}). The Uniform Gate learns comparable per-layer variation (L3\,=\,0.777, L27\,=\,1.000) despite lacking turn conditioning; the structure thus arises from LoRA$+$GAR pressure (Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}a). Does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent the intended behavior?
15	
16	\subsection{PCGrad Gradient Direction Conflict Exclusion}\label{subsec:pcgrad}
17	
18	NTP and GAR gradients oppose in 68--79\% of layers (cosine similarity $-$0.089$\to$$-$0.042 across epochs), with NTP magnitude dominance growing from 7.5$\times$ to 14.7$\times$ (full gradient statistics in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). If this direction conflict prevents GAR convergence, eliminating it via PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects conflicting gradients onto the other's normal plane, should prevent the collapse. To our knowledge, this is the first application of PCGrad as a \emph{diagnostic} tool rather than an optimization method.
19	
20	PCGrad shifts cosine similarity to oscillate around zero, yet TF metrics are unchanged. AR GAR \emph{worsens}: 0.064$\to$0.030 ($p$\,=\,0.002; Figure~\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-gradient}). By \emph{modus tollens}: removing gradient direction conflict does not prevent collapse; therefore it is not the primary cause. PCGrad eliminates direction conflict but does not address magnitude imbalance (NTP dominates GAR by 7.5--14.7$\times$); the worsening under PCGrad may partly reflect projection side-effects rather than confirming that direction conflict is entirely absent as a factor. Cross-model analysis confirms this pattern: Llama-3.1-8B gradients are near-orthogonal (cos\,=\,+0.005) with 25.7$\times$ NTP magnitude dominance (Table~\ref{tab:gradient-crossmodel}).
21	
22	\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\label{subsec:dist-shift}
23	
24	The TF-AR GAR gap persists across all variants (Table~\ref{tab:five-way}). GAR-Dongre provides a control: it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, evidence that the shift selectively destroys concentrated last-token attention. The mechanism is exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled,zhang2019bridging,arora2022exposure}: during training the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. GAR optimizes a distributional property with no autoregressive counterpart, explaining why TF GAR-ours remains near $\tau$\,=\,0.5 while AR GAR collapses (77--87\% per-turn decay; Figure~\ref{fig:attention-heatmap}; per-configuration AR trajectories in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ar-gar-dynamics}; scaling analysis in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-eb-scaling}).
25	
26	\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}
27	
28	We test specificity with Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC), the cosine similarity between adjacent-turn hidden states. TF/AR TRC is 0.334/0.332 for ThreadGate (gap $+$0.002) and 0.349/0.411 for LoRA-only (gap $-$0.062, reversed); exposure bias thus disrupts a specific routing behavior while leaving representations intact. Cross-model evaluation confirms: GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift on both Llama (1.002$\times$) and Qwen (1.28$\times$) vs.\ the order-of-magnitude GAR-ours collapse.
29	
30	\begin{figure}[t]
31	  \centering
32	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_ss_multiseed.pdf}
33	  \caption{Multi-seed AR GAR under scheduled sampling ($\beta$\,=\,0 and $\beta$\,=\,5, three seeds). Steering nearly eliminates seed variance (std: 0.047$\to$0.001).}
34	  \label{fig:ss-multiseed}
35	\end{figure}
36	
37	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}\label{subsec:cross-model}
38	
39	We replicate on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} (Base, ThreadGate, LoRA-only; $N$\,=\,20, 4 turns). TF GAR-ours is 0.484 (TG) / 0.481 (LoRA-only); gating contributes zero benefit. The AR distribution is heavy-tailed (coefficient of variation, CoV\,$>$\,100\%); we report medians: 0.018 (TG and LoRA-only), 0.009 (Base), yielding gaps of 27$\times$ and 26$\times$ (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}a in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-llama}; per-turn values in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-perturn-detail}). Gate profiles differ qualitatively (Figure~\ref{fig:crossmodel}b): Qwen attenuates early layers (L1\,=\,0.793) while Llama dips at late layers (L25\,=\,0.719), an architecture-dependent pattern, but convergence toward identity is universal (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-viz}). The gap is substantial on both architectures, consistent with exposure bias as architecture-independent.
40	
41	Scheduled sampling confirms cross-model generalization: Llama SS compresses the gap from ${\sim}$27$\times$ to 2.9$\times$ (TF GAR 0.477, AR GAR 0.166), weaker than Qwen's ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ likely due to fewer training epochs (2 vs.\ 3). The phase transition is architecture-dependent: $\beta$\,=\,3 triggers recovery on Qwen SS (0.593) but not Llama SS (0.173); Llama requires $\beta$\,=\,5 (GAR 0.791, exceeding Qwen SS 0.717; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-llama-ss}). Cross-scale validation on Qwen-2.5-1.5B confirms the same pattern (79.8\% GAR decay, $\beta$-steering recovery to 0.715; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-1.5b}).
42	
43

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Multi-turn dialogue is central to how large language models are deployed, from customer service to tutoring to long-form reasoning. Yet a persistent failure accompanies even the most fluent generation: as conversations grow longer, models pay progressively less attention to the system-prompt goals that define their task. \citet{dongre2026attention} measured significant Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) decline across 12 models over extended conversations; in our own evaluation, goal attention drops by over 70\% within just five turns. Crucially, this is not a representation problem: residual stream probes confirm that goal information persists (AUC\,=\,0.98). The model retains the knowledge of what it should attend to; it simply stops attending.
4	
5	Existing remedies address this decay at inference time. Split-softmax \citep{li2024measuring} separates instruction from conversation tokens; StreamingLLM \citep{xiao2024streaming} retains anchor tokens; Gated Attention \citep{qiu2025gated} applies per-head sigmoid gates within a single forward pass. A natural alternative is to intervene during training: optimize goal attention directly through auxiliary losses trained alongside next-token prediction (NTP). Yet this strategy rests on an untested assumption: attention patterns learned under teacher forcing (TF) transfer to autoregressive (AR) generation. Classical exposure bias research \citep{bengio2015scheduled,lamb2016professor} has shown that TF creates a training--inference distribution mismatch for RNN sequence models, but whether this mismatch extends to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers remains unknown.
6	
7	We test this assumption and find it fails. We introduce a controlled five-way testbed (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, and Base) designed not as competing solutions but as a causal exclusion chain (\S\ref{sec:comparison-design}), where each adjacent pair isolates one candidate failure mechanism. The result is unambiguous: every variant converges to target GAR under teacher forcing, yet goal attention collapses by an order of magnitude once the model generates its own context (Figure~\ref{fig:headline}). The exclusion chain traces this collapse to exposure bias, the training--inference distribution mismatch, ruling out gradient direction conflict (PCGrad removes conflict yet \emph{worsens} AR GAR), gating mechanism (Uniform Gate matches ThreadGate), and parameter capacity (LoRA-only shows the same pattern). Inference-time $\beta$-steering confirms that the collapse reflects attention misallocation rather than representation degradation; scheduled sampling bridges the gap by exposing the model to its own generations during training.
8	
9	\begin{figure*}[!ht]
10	  \centering
11	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_headline.pdf}
12	  \caption{Exposure bias collapses auxiliary attention losses. \textbf{(a)}~Goal attention decays 85--90\% across turns under AR generation on both Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B. \textbf{(b)}~Under TF evaluation all variants reach GAR$\approx$0.50, but AR generation collapses GAR by 8--17$\times$ (log scale). \textbf{(c)}~Scheduled sampling compresses the TF--AR gap from 7.8$\times$ to 1.3$\times$ (Qwen) and ${\sim}$27$\times$ to 2.9$\times$ (Llama).}
13	  \label{fig:headline}
14	\end{figure*}
15	
16	Our contributions are:
17	\begin{enumerate}\raggedright
18	    \item \textbf{Exposure bias characterization.} To our knowledge, the first empirical demonstration that exposure bias collapses auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers, with an order-of-magnitude TF--AR gap reproduced across two architectures (Qwen-2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B) and model scales (1.5B--8B).
19	    \item \textbf{Causal exclusion testbed.} A five-way diagnostic design, informed by seven loss-formulation iterations, that isolates the failure mechanism via causal exclusion, including a PCGrad \emph{modus tollens} argument ruling out gradient direction conflict ($p$\,=\,0.002, effect reverses).
20	    \item \textbf{Attention misallocation diagnosis.} Inference-time $\beta$-steering recovers goal attention with modest perplexity cost, evidence that the collapse is an attention routing failure, not a representational one.
21	    \item \textbf{Scheduled sampling as remedy.} The first application of scheduled sampling to auxiliary attention objectives in multi-turn transformers, compressing the TF--AR gap to ${\sim}$1.3$\times$ on Qwen and ${\sim}$2.9$\times$ on Llama with no perplexity degradation, robust across three seeds.
22	\end{enumerate}
23	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\looseness=-1 NTP self-recovers because prediction is the same task at both regimes \citep{he2019exposure}; GAR optimizes a \emph{global property} (attention allocation to goal tokens) with no autoregressive counterpart. The collapse is selective: GAR-ours collapses by an order of magnitude while GAR-Dongre shows negligible shift (${\leq}$1.3$\times$). Per-head analysis confirms selectivity: heads with higher TF GAR shift most under AR ($r$\,=\,0.743; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-kl}). Any auxiliary attention objective targeting distributional properties rather than prediction itself should face the same barrier under teacher forcing.
4	
5	\looseness=-1 Our exclusion identifies exposure bias as a primary contributing factor, but cannot fully rule out KV cache drift (\S\ref{sec:limitations}). The PCGrad exclusion rules out direction conflict but not magnitude imbalance (7.5--25.7$\times$ NTP dominance; \citealp{chen2018gradnorm}). The GAR--compliance correlation is weak ($r$\,=\,0.200; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ir}), reinforcing GAR's role as a diagnostic probe. Our $\beta$-steering is likewise a \emph{diagnostic}, distinct from inference-time remedies such as split-softmax \citep{li2024measuring} and Rhea \citep{hong2025rhea}. SS and inference-time fixes are complementary: SS modifies representations without inference cost; inference-time methods cannot recover patterns never learned. At $\beta$\,=\,5 the two converge (SS\,=\,0.717 vs.\ TG\,=\,0.727).
6	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related}
2	
3	\paragraph{Multi-turn dialogue and attention decay.}
4	Multi-turn coherence has been a persistent challenge from early dialogue systems \citep{li2016persona,roller2021recipes,zhang2020dialogpt} through modern long-context models \citep{xu2022goldfish}, with benchmarks such as MT-Bench \citep{zheng2024judging} and MT-Bench-101 \citep{bai2024mtbench101} continuing to expose quality gaps at later turns \citep{laban2025lost,liu2026intent}. Most relevant to our work, \citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Accessibility Ratio (GAR) and showed that attention to goal tokens decays 27--48\% across 12 models while residual probes retain goal information (AUC\,=\,0.98). We observe a steeper 70.8\% decline on Qwen-2.5-7B. \citet{li2024measuring} independently quantified instruction instability and proposed split-softmax as an inference-time remedy. Our work differs in scope: we diagnose \emph{why} training-time attention optimization fails to transfer to inference, and provide the first training-time mitigation.
5	
6	\paragraph{Gating and attention manipulation.}
7	Residual gating spans highway networks \citep{srivastava2015highway}, ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}, layer pruning \citep{gromov2024unreasonable,fan2020layerdrop}, and adaptive depth \citep{schuster2022calm,raposo2024mixture}. Most relevant is Gated Attention \citep{qiu2025gated}: per-head sigmoid gates achieving MMLU \citep{hendrycks2021measuring} $+$2.03 at 15B scale; ThreadGate differs in conditioning on turn position. On the analysis side, head specialization \citep{clark2019what,voita2019analyzing,kovaleva2019revealing}, positional bias calibration \citep{liu2024lost,xiao2024streaming}, and attention steering (PASTA; \citealp{zhang2024tell}) address inference behavior; our $\beta$-steering serves a diagnostic role. PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad} and CAGrad \citep{liu2021cagrad} treat gradient conflict as the root cause; we show direction conflict is a symptom, not the primary cause.
8	
9	\paragraph{Exposure bias in sequence models.}
10	Teacher forcing creates a distributional mismatch between training and inference \citep{wiseman2016sequence,arora2022exposure}. Scheduled sampling \citep{bengio2015scheduled} introduced curriculum-based mixing; subsequent work proposed sequence-level objectives \citep{ranzato2016sequence}, adversarial regularization \citep{lamb2016professor}, self-recovery analysis \citep{he2019exposure}, bridging strategies for neural machine translation (NMT; \citealp{zhang2019bridging}), and search-based training \citep{leblond2018searnn}. \citet{mihaylova2019scheduled} extended scheduled sampling to transformers for single-turn NMT. \citet{dou2019attention} proposed attention forcing (supervising cross-attention during NMT), and \citet{dou2022parallel} extended it with KL-divergence switching. Our work differs: (i)~time-scheduled sampling (linearly increasing $\rho$), not data-dependent; (ii)~self-attention in multi-turn dialogue, not cross-attention in NMT; (iii)~auxiliary attention objectives, not primary translation loss. No prior work examines exposure bias on \emph{auxiliary} attention objectives or provides multi-turn transformer mitigation; we fill both gaps.
11	
```

On the analysis side, head specialization \\citep{clark2019what,voita2019analyzing,kovaleva2019revealing}, positional bias calibration \\citep{liu2024lost,xiao2024streaming}, and attention steering (PASTA; \\citealp{zhang2024tell}) address inference behavior; our $\\beta$-steering serves a diagnostic role. PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} and CAGrad \\citep{liu2021cagrad} treat gradient conflict as the root cause; we show direction conflict is a symptom, not the primary cause.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure bias in sequence models.}\nTeacher forcing creates a distributional mismatch between training and inference \\citep{wiseman2016sequence,arora2022exposure}. Scheduled sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled} introduced curriculum-based mixing; subsequent work proposed sequence-level objectives \\citep{ranzato2016sequence}, adversarial regularization \\citep{lamb2016professor}, self-recovery analysis \\citep{he2019exposure}, bridging strategies for neural machine translation (NMT; \\citealp{zhang2019bridging}), and search-based training \\citep{leblond2018searnn}. \\citet{mihaylova2019scheduled} extended scheduled sampling to transformers for single-turn NMT. \\citet{dou2019attention} proposed attention forcing (supervising cross-attention during NMT), and \\citet{dou2022parallel} extended it with KL-divergence switching. Our work differs: (i)~time-scheduled sampling (linearly increasing $\\rho$), not data-dependent; (ii)~self-attention in multi-turn dialogue, not cross-attention in NMT; (iii)~auxiliary attention objectives, not primary translation loss. No prior work examines exposure bias on \\emph{auxiliary} attention objectives or provides multi-turn transformer mitigation; we fill both gaps.\n",
      "numLines": 11,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 11
    }
